#7406. Senior Contracts Manager at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We are looking for a Senior Contracts Manager to take ownership of vendor contracting at Supabase. You will be the go-to contracts partner for everything we buy.

This is a hands-on, high-autonomy role with real ownership, for an experienced negotiator who takes pride in making procurement faster while managing risk. You will negotiate directly, build the playbooks and workflows that let the business self-serve on low-risk deals, and escalate the right issues to Legal at the right time. We work AI-first in Legal: you will use Claude and Ironclad every day to move faster and with greater accuracy. Your work will shape how procurement and vendor contracting scale as Supabase grows.

Review, draft, and negotiate a high volume of vendor agreements, including MSAs, order forms, SOWs, SaaS and cloud subscription terms, DPAs, and NDAs.

Serve as the single point of contact for all vendor contracting matters raised by FinOps, IT, Security, and business owners, from intake through signature, renewals, amendments, disputes, and offboarding. As capacity allows, you will also support the broader Commercial Legal team on other matters.

Apply and further build out our playbook and risk framework independently, and escalate to the Assistant General Counsel, Commercial where a deal falls outside approved positions or raises novel legal risk.

Review AI vendor terms, such as training on our data, output ownership, and model changes.

Build and scale vendor contracting operations

Build out and maintain our vendor playbook in Claude, so the whole team works from the same guidance and low-risk deals move without bottlenecks.

Design and improve intake, triage, and approval workflows with FinOps.

Own the vendor and procurement workflow in Ironclad, including intake, templates, approvals, and the repository, working with Legal Ops, who own the platform. Use it to track renewals and notice windows with FinOps and flag spend concentration risks.

Create training and self-serve resources for business partners, and raise the visibility of the contracts function as a business enabler.

6 to 8+ years of experience negotiating procurement, vendor, or technology transactions contracting, ideally buy-side at a U.S. SaaS or technology company, with deep knowledge of SaaS, cloud, and software licensing terms.

A track record of negotiating directly with vendors and their counsel, independently and at pace.

Experience designing contracting processes that are consistent, transparent, and easy for the business to follow.

Clear writing and speaking, with the ability to explain legal and commercial risk plainly and the judgment to know when to hold a position, approve a reasonable risk, or escalate.

Comfort using AI tools, Claude in particular, with sound judgment about when to rely on outputs, how to verify them, and how to protect confidential information.

A degree in a relevant field (for example, law, economics, or political science), though we value experience over the specific degree.

Sell-side experience negotiating customer, partner, or reseller agreements for a SaaS or technology company, which helps you see vendor terms from the other side.

Hands-on experience with Ironclad or a similar CLM tool (Juro, Docusign CLM) or building AI-assisted legal workflows, ideally with some exposure to AI governance.

Experience working across jurisdictions or with distributed, international teams.

Experience at a fast-growing, developer-focused, cloud, security, or open source company.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7405. Inside Nike’s plan to turn creators into brand builders

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Nike’s turnaround plan gives creators a bigger role in building the brand.

That became clear at its investor day earlier this week (November 17), where execs spent plenty of time on getting closer to athletes and stretched the definition of one to take in far more people.

“When we say athlete, we mean athlete in the broadest sense possible. If you have a body, you are an athlete,” CEO Eliott Hill told investors.

That definition puts everyday participants alongside elite competitors. Separately, Nike is looking to bring creators, cultural voices and local communities closer to the brand as part of its growth strategy. Together, these moves are part of “Full Stride Ahead”, the strategy Nike is counting on to get back to sustainable, profitable growth. Doing so rests on three pillars the brand’s top brass call “Epic”, “Connected” and “Fast.”. They cover product innovation, consumer relationships and speed to market. Connected is where creates and athletes come in.

Brand president Amy Montagne said Nike is changing how it spends its marketing dollars, with more weight on “grassroots connections, local creators and relevant cultural voices”. She said the goal is to reach communities through “the right people, in the right places and at the right moments”.

The splashy camopaigns and superstar deals will carry on. Next to them, Nike is building a looser network of local influence, informed by research across more than 10 markets where it quizzed athletes, retailers, creators and tastemakers about how tastes shift from place to place. Local teams will get more say over product creation, assortments and how the brand shows up in each market. The shift is particularly clear in China, where Nike acknowledged that its previous model was too centralized toward its headquarters in Beaverton, Oregon. It is now moving towards more locally driven product creation and giving teams closer to shoppers greater influence over decisions.

That means changes for creators too. Nike wants their help reading an audience and translating product into local culture, a job that starts well before any campaign lives. The company plans to expand its use of creator networks, entertainment partnerships and athlete-led storytelling.

“Putting the onus on the regions is the right way of thinking as long as it goes deep enough,” said James Kirkham, founder of brand consultancy Iconic. “With JD Sports we worked on ‘second cities’ like Liverpool, Rotterdam and Lyon (these are often port cities for various historic cultural hot spot reasons), which tend to get to culture first.”

The trends in Liverpool are very different from those just 30 miles down the road in Manchester, he continued, right down to the shape and fit of some jogging bottoms and a brand that lumps the two together has already lost so knowing that sheer nuance and level of detail and flagging it is key to proper authentic hits.

Nike’s football plans show this is supposed to work. The company built the strategy around a consumer archetype it calls the “Mad Baller” — a football obsessed teenager who plays the game, watches it and dresses the part. Nike is chasing that kid across what it calls pitch, stands and street which ties elite players and performance boots to fan culture, fashion, creators and grassroots football.

Some of that was on display over the summer. At the World Cup, its “Rip the Script” campaign got 5.7 billion content views, according to the investor day presentation. During it, execs also highlighted how a new generation of creators brought its sotires into football culture generating hundreds of millions of views. Nike then spread the story across individual athlete content, creator collabs, product drops, retail experiences and local tournaments.

Sponsored athletes are in a line for a similar rethink. A player’s value to Nike used to rest mostly on performance and visibility during tournaments. Now, the company wants their training, persona;lities and fan relationships to keep the brand in people’s feeds all year. That also opens up collaborations with creators that reach well beyond the athletes own sport.

Nike seems more interested in bringing creators into how the brand gets built than in buying sponsored posts. A running creator may understand amateur runners better than an ad agency does. A local fashion figure in Lagos or Seoul may have a sharper sense of how sport feeds into streetwear in their city than a global marketing team in Beaverton. People like that can spot trends early, shape storytelling and turn passing interest into actual participation.

Plenty of that work will still look like ordinary campaign and content distribution, of course, and most creators won’t become long-term strategic partners. Yet Nike’s growing use of creator networks could become a competitive advantage, particularly when creators have real standing in a sporting or cultural community. For these creators, the opportunity could extend beyond sponsored content into partnerships involving product, communities and intellectual property.

Nike offered another example of this approach through its Studio Fleece launch. The initiative brought together more than 100 athletes, entertainers, creators and stylists, combining product seeding, creative participation and Nike By You experiences. The collection was distributed across more than 6,000 retail outlets. Nike described it as the largest new apparel launch in its history, with the highest proportion of sales made at full price of any fleece launch.

None of this matters if it doesn’t contribute to sales so Nike is focusing on five priority sports: running, training, global football, basketball and outdoor plus sportswear. More often than not those efforts will be focused on 23 countries that Nike expects to deliver about 90% of its growth opportunity between fiscal 2028 and 2031. The goal is around $5 billion in extra revenue by fiscal 2031, compared with fiscal 2027. Nike expeccts about $4 billion of that to come from the five sports and about one billion to come from sportswear.

Running was the proof point the investor day presentations kept coming back to. The category has delivered nine consecutive quarters of growth from the first quarter of its fiscal year in 2025 through to the first for 2027, including six straight quarters of double-digit growth. That’s around £1 billion revenue for the business.

Nike’s Montagne described the wider goal as building “a repeatable engine to create demand,” and that’s the phrase investors are likely to hold the company to. Indeed, Nike wants a model it can roll out across sports and markets deliberately so it isn’t waiting for the occasional cultural moment to land.

A bigger role in a brand’s strategy raises new questions for creators, athletes and the people who represent them. A creator who helps a brand understand a community and build demand there might reasonably look for something beyond a campaign fee. An athlete who helps build a content platform or a product line could push to share in the long-term value they help create. And as brand-run realtor networks get more sophisticated, ownership of the audience, the content and the IP becomes a bigger part of any negotiation.

Investor Days are always about a company’s growth plans so no surprise that compensation models weren’t on the agenda. Those conversations are more likely to happen deal by deal, and in turn create another boon for the cottage industry of agencies, talent managers and creator businesses that have emerged around the creator economy to put together partnerships that account for the wider value creators and athletes bring.

“If I were managing a creator right now then I’d tell them to play up everything specific about themselves: their expertise, their obsessions, their accent, their postcode. Get into the nerdiest detail you’ve got and bring every bit of it to life,” said Iconic’s Kirkham “The deals Nike is describing will go to deep experts with a real local standing and a million views on last decade’s platforms won’t get anyone really bothered or doing the marketing for you any more, you need to go beautifully deep and nuanced.”

Nike has the scale, product and the distribution. The cultural pull and local knowledge it wants increasingly sit outside the company, with creators and athletes it’s now trying to bring closer to the centre of its business.

#7401. In Graphic Detail: Welcome to the agentic commerce quarter

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Holiday 2026 is shaping up as the first season where the shopping agent actually pays — solidifying Q4 as the agentic commerce quarter.

OpenAI launched ChatGPT wallet this week to push agent purchase through retailer checkouts, alongside a commerce partner playbook aimed at helping businesses prepare for a future of more agent-led purchase. Google began rolling its UCP-powered checkout in Canada through AI Mode and Gemini. Amazon blocked Meta’s Muse AI agent from its retail site. Walmart lets customers cap an agent’s spending at $50, $100 or $200. And Bain reckons roughly one in four online shoppers plan to start their holiday shopping journeys on an AI platform.

Put together, agentic commerce is having a growth spurt even if it’s still a while from growing up.

Agentic commerce is moving from hypothetical to commercial opportunity, with platforms and retailers beginning to position themselves for a new way of shopping. U.S. retail e-commerce sales completed through end-to-end agentic commerce are forecast to reach $1.44 billion this year, according to eMarketer. That figure is expected to increase 10.6% to $1.59 billion in 2027, a further 11% increase to $1.76 billion in 2028 and reach $2.17 trillion by 2030.

Marketers are already adapting their strategies to account for AI agents, although the focus so far seems to be making brands discoverable, rather than enabling agents to buy from them.

According to Gartner’s 2026 Marketing Strategy and Engagement Survey, 61% of senior marketing leaders have adapted content and SEO/AEO to improve machine interpretability. A further 42% are monitoring and correcting misinformation about their brands in AI-generated outputs, while 39% have tailored product information, pricing or offers for machine and agent decision-making.

But the same survey found that fewer have taken proper steps yet to accommodate agent-led transactions. Around a third (30%) of marketers have designed interfaces or workflows for agent transactions, while 32% manage machines and agents as a distinct audience alongside human customers.

Brands might be getting ready for agents, but most consumers aren’t yet comfortable letting one spend their money unsupervised.

According to U.S. identity and access management company Okta’s Customer Identity Trends Report 2026, when consumers were asked how likely they’d be to let an AI agent complete a purchase without reviewing it first, only a third (31%) said they were very or rather likely to. At the same time, 67% of those surveyed said they were unlikely to let an AI agent finish a purchase without review, while 41% said they wouldn’t at all.

Of course, there are exceptions. Early adopters of the tech are far more trusting. Nearly two thirds (62%) said they’d let an AI agent complete a purchase without checking, and 42% said they’d be very likely to.

The gap suggests the hard part of agentic commerce may not be getting the right tech, but instead building the right amount of trust.

Shoppers might not be handing over full control to the checkout just yet, but AI tools are already influencing where they go to buy, and the visitors those tools send are more likely to spend.

In September, shoppers who arrived at U.S. retail e-commerce sites from AI tools converted 90% better than those from other channels, according to Adobe Digital Insights. That’s the highest level Adobe has recorded, and a sharp turnaround from August 2025 when AI traffic converted 9% worse than other channels.

For major e-commerce businesses, agentic commerce could have implications beyond how products are sold. It could also change how retailers make money from helping shoppers to discover them.

Amazon, for example, generated $68.5 billion in net sales from its online stores during Q3 2026, alongside $46.5 billion from third-party seller services, and $20 billion from ad services, according to Enders Analysis.

As agentic commerce becomes a more natural part of the consumer journey, shoppers may increasingly rely on agents to identify and recommend products rather than browse sponsored listings. It could affect Amazon’s business in different ways: its fulfilment infrastructure could support transactions initiated elsewhere, while its advertising business could face pressure if fewer shoppers encounter paid placements on its platform.

#7396. How Celsius doubled down on sports marketing campaigns in 2026

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

Energy drink brand Celsius is rapidly expanding its footprint across the sports landscape.

The company is increasingly leveraging partnerships with major sports leagues, college athletics and high-profile athletic events to connect with health-conscious consumers. In 2026 alone, Celsius has announced several sponsorships and marketing activations across professional and amateur sporting events.

In August, Celsius also became the official energy drink sponsor of ESPN’s College GameDay. The multi-year partnership kicked off for the 2026 season at major campus stops, with activations including run clubs and tailgates. For its college football NIL (name, image and likeness) integration, Celsius partnered with college football analyst Kirk Herbstreit, alongside a collegiate roster that included Michigan quarterback Bryce Underwood and Oregon quarterback Dante Moore. To close out the year, Celsius is delving deeper into the burgeoning world of Formula 1. The energy drink brand signed British professional driver Jessica Hawkins ahead of the 2026 Las Vegas Grand Prix this month. By aligning its brand with active lifestyles and high-performance athletics, Celsius aims to solidify its position as a better-for-you functional beverage for elite competitors and everyday fitness enthusiasts.

Many professional sporting events are sponsored by major hydration or alcohol brands. However, Celsius says its origins as a better-for-you alternative to sugary energy drinks position it as a product that can support fitness enthusiasts and athletes.

Running has also quickly become a focus of Celsius’ sports marketing strategy. Most recently, the brand returned with a Club Celsius pop-up at the New York City Marathon, which included a multi-day takeover in Brooklyn along the race route. Throughout the fall, the brand also sponsored Diplo’s Run Club stops in New York, Chicago and Los Angeles. In a related category, fitness events and competitions are a growing marketing opportunity for Celsius. Earlier this year, the brand also became the official global energy drink partner of the fitness competition ATHX Games.

Kyle Watson, CMO of Celsius, told Modern Retail that with the ongoing Live. Fit. Go. campaign at the center of the brand, sports is a natural and effective space for Celsius to show up. The 360 campaign, which targets active consumers, launched in 2025 with TV, paid ads, organic social, out-of-home and experiential activations.

“What differentiates us is the breadth of our presence,” Watson said. “Celsius isn’t defined by a single sport; we’re building meaningful connections across the entire sports landscape.”

That has meant heavy investment across collegiate sports for the past few years, largely through strategic NIL partnerships with athletes across football, basketball and gymnastics. “This year, we took that presence to the next level through our multi-year partnership with ESPN’s College GameDay,” said Watson. Through Celsius Combine, a football-inspired fan event, along with campus run clubs, Watson said the brand is offering immersive experiences that embody the Live. Fit. Go. campaign.

More and more, brands are using sporting events to do official or proximity marketing to capture fan excitement. This has become a popular tactic during big events like the Super Bowl and even college sports tournaments.

Anjali Bal, associate professor of marketing at Babson College, said today’s consumers are highly invested in sporting events, which makes them prime moments for marketing campaigns.

“Adrenaline and psychological responses are high” at these events, Bal said. “When you tie this to team loyalty and the desire to belong, consumers are more open to messaging and brands; they align with their values and their teams.”

That said, Watson said Celsius views its sports strategy as extending beyond traditional sporting events. For example, Watson said, “Through our multi-year partnership with Aston Martin Aramco, one of the most ambitious teams in Formula 1, we’re showing up at key races and connecting with a premium, global audience.” Watson added that Celsius’ Formula 1 presence is another example of how the brand is trying to credibly align with elite athletes and competitors across various disciplines. “Because our product is a drink for athletes, regardless of the sport,” Watson added.

Looking ahead, Watson said Celsius will continue to evolve its sports strategy to reach people more often and in more meaningful ways. “In 2027, consumers can expect even more impactful partnerships with leading teams, elite athletes and high-performance programs, all designed to bring the Live. Fit. Go. mentality to life,” Watson said.

#7394. Mail Metro Media’s next creator play is representing them

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Mail Metro Media now thinks “more like a talent agency” and commissions “like a broadcaster”, according to Hannah Blake, its managing director of creator and new media. Its creators have, in other words, become the business.

Audiences tune in for them, brands pay to reach those audiences, and the deals have grown from around £5,000 apiece to worth seven figures in October. Holding on to those creators is now as important as finding them since any one who leaves for an outside agent can take the audience and advertisers along too.

Blake spelled out that risk on stage at the Digiday Media Strategies London event in October.

“The huge risk is that we make these people super famous and then they go and get signed by a different agent,” she said.

So in September the ad sales arm for the Daily Mail hired David White from Whalar as head of creators, with a brief to build an in-house talent agency that represents its creators and signs outside talent too. White was the first hire for the company’s Stars business in New York, the talent management arm that handles creators’ careers beyond brand deals and was also a senior talent manager there.

What the business ultimately ends up with is still open. Blake described something that “potentially could look more like a talent agency and an employer,” and she hopes to have it in market by the end of the year. The final shape matters because running a talent agency would be a big change of job for a newspaper group’s ad sales team. It exists to sell audiences to brands while an agent’s job is to get the best deal for the talent. Mail Metro Media would be trying to do both at once.

That’s the potted history of how Mail Metro Media reached this point. The full story is a lot more nuanced.

It started four years ago when Daily Mail launched Eliza, a social-only brand for young women fronted by six fashion and beauty creators. Its hit format, “Guess Which Outfit’s Most Expensive”, puts two women on the street, one in a £10,000 outfit and the other in a high-street copy, and leaves viewers to guess which is which. It kept going viral, people kept coming back and advertisers soon followed. That’s despite it being relatively small next to the rest of the Mail’s portfolio, and yet “we were doing lots of direct advertisers relationships quite quickly”, Blake said. Fast forward to now,  and brands often book content on Eliza and then want its creators posting on their own accounts too, which Mail Metro Media packages into the deal.

The Mail’s own TikTok was a different story. The Mail and Metro launched on the platform around the same time as Eliza and the former became the biggest news publisher there, with 20 million followers, on the back of rapid-fire news clips. But the money didn’t follow.  “The huge issue was there was no monetization there,” Blake said, even though publishers had widely expected TikTok to start paying them for views. Advertisers weren’t keen either, Every rival was posting the same kind of clips and the Daily Mail couldn’t build a brand into footage it had brought in from elsewhere.

Together, they showed the publisher that the money was in shows it owned and creators audiences came back for. It shifted course accordingly, and over the past year it relaunched the Mail and Metro on social around repeatable shows it owns, such as the sports debate show “Dugout Debate” with creators at the front and helping shape them. Blake said the operation now pulls 5 billion monthly video views across 10 brands and signed its first seven-figure deal last month. With that success comes a new kind of risk. The creators fronting those shows have built followings of their own and “now some of the superstars are beginning to emerge,” Blake said. Left unchecked, the publisher ends up making stars for outside agencies to sign.

In some ways, the publisher has already been acting like an agent of sorts. Alongside its core bundle where brands pay one flat rate for a run of content with “no differentiation between the talent,” Blake said it has spent the past year selling individual creators on their own channels. It calls this add-on “Creator Amp.” One example is Mimi Yates, a young journalist who fronts much of the news output and has built a following of her own. Brands can book her as part of a wider campaign or on her own, and on their personal accounts the creators “all set their own rates,” Blake said.

That arrangement was easy to manage while the talent was still unknown.

“When we started, our creators were all emerging,” Blake said. With bigger names now coming through, the publisher wants something sturdier. “We are now at the point where we’re beginning to formalize that relationship a bit more,” she said, and hiring White was the first step.

He will recruit from two places. One is from its own staff. The other is outside talent Blake described as “the next generation of emerging news and entertainment talent”.

Moves like this bring some awkward conversations with them. Daily Mail’s creators are salaried and Nathan Aspell, director of the New Media division, said the company owns the IP they develop. Blake described those same creators setting their own rates on their own channels. How the money is split between the employer and agent will go a long way to deciding whether a breakout star sticks around.

Axel Springer’s version hands journalists a share of the financial results in return for production, marketing and distribution. Daily Mail hasn’t said what its agency will charge.

Then there’s the small matter of journalism. The same person can read the headlines and sell the mascara, and the publisher would be both their employer and their agent. That’s a lot of hats for one newsroom.

White’s first signings will show how far the publisher plans to take this venture. Blake thinks publishers are headed this way regardless. “I actually think it’s just a byproduct of where the opportunity is,” she said.

For a lot of publishers, signing talent still feels like someone else’s business and talk of newsrooms turning into agencies tends to raise eyebrows. Blake sees it as following the money, which increasingly sits with “people-centric IP that exists across multiple different touchpoints”.

Plenty of rivals are following it too.  Axel Springer launched its own spin on the same idea last month, while Hearst did so earlier this week. Increasingly, publishers see a talent operation as the next step in their latest pivot to video, which runs on personalities whose audiences, and the ad money chasing them, follow them wherever they go.

“Publishers are responding to the audience’s desire for personality-led formats while limiting strategic dependence on individual journalists and managing talent,” said Laura Darcey, research analyst at Enders Analysis.

Editor’s note: An earlier version of this article said David White ran Whalar’s Stars business in New York. He was the first hire for the division in New York.

Consulting giants Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

With shareholders, an $895 billion compute bill and a costly free tier, Anthropic’s no-ads pledge may have a shelf life.

Creator-led production is the fastest-growing segment of the influencer economy. We explain what’s driving investment in this practice.

#7389. Last of the old school buyers, Publicis Media vet John Muszynski prepares to retire

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

He’s equally comfortable at the negotiating table or on skates in an ice rink.

He doesn’t do it himself but he knows principal media is part of the business.

He’s said to have once humbled the CEO of a major cable network group by holding back $200 million of his clients’ media spend when that CEO tried to bully him into big price increases.

He’s the longest-serving head of investment in media. And now, John Muszynski, legendary media investment executive with Publicis Media, plans to retire.

After 45 years at one holding company and negotiating nearly as many upfronts over that span, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Chicago-based Muszynski, who oversees decision-making on what Publicis estimates to be 40% of the U.S. video marketplace inventory, is arguably the last of the old-school heads of investment that have seen the upfronts morph into the byzantine bazaar it is today.

“For nearly five decades, I’ve had a front row seat to the industry’s transformation,” Muszynski told Digiday. “It’s gone from three networks to always-on content spanning the entire digital ecosystem. It’s been fascinating to be a part of it all.”

The avid ice hockey fan, hunter and fisherman (when he’s not negotiating) got his start in 1981 as a planner for Leo Burnett’s burgeoning media unit, handling longtime Publicis clients including Kimberly-Clark, McDonald’s and Kellogg’s. That unit eventually became standalone media agency Starcom, for which he rose to CEO of Starcom USA from 2005-2009. He then helped to build Spark Foundry from a 65-person boutique into a 3,000-person media agency.

In 2016, Publicis formed Publicis Media Exchange, which harnessed the collective buying clout of what was then Starcom, Mediavest, Spark, Zenith, Optimedia and Blue 449 media agencies. He was the lead negotiator when that formed, and became chairman of the group in 2022.

The execs who sat across from him at the negotiating table said they always felt they got a fair shake — in part because Muszynski always came prepared, was knowledgable and true to his word.

“John has been a tremendous influence on generations of marketers, agency team members and publishers,” said Mark Marshall, chairman of global advertising and partnerships at NBCUniversal. “He has always put his clients’ objectives at the forefront and pushed all of us to deliver for those clients. He is the epitome of ‘a handshake is his word’.”

Rita Ferro, president, global advertising, disney, was even more personal in her assessment of working with Muszynski. “John is a legend in this business and from the moment we met, he became a trusted partner and more importantly a dear friend,” she said.

Sean Moran, COO at Evergreen Trading agency, who negotiated with Muszynski during his 24 years as an ad sales exec with Viacom until 2020, thinks of Muszynski as a contemporary of the old-school buyers of the TV business, from Grey Advertising’s Jon Mandel to UM’s Donna Wolfe, and from Omnicom’s John Swift to GroupM’s Rino Scanzoni.

“When I was running MTV and we had a big upfront event, and had to get people there, if I could get John Muszynski to fly in and come to my event, that was a huge win,” said Moran. ”He represented so much gravitas. Obviously he controlled a lot of budgets, but it was also because he was so smart and yet had such empathy for good people. You couldn’t bullshit him. He was a good Midwestern guy who could talk about a great deal over over a steak, but he did his homework and knew his numbers.”

Moran said Muszynski often set the marketplace as “the main controller” of the upfronts. “One of his negotiating tactics was, ‘Yeah, I haven’t seen everything, but boy, it looks down’,” said Moran. “If he respected your inventory, you could do a deal with him fairly quickly and cut through the chaff. He was known to pull that with one or two people that were at the front of the line, and protect his clients. He would therefore set the marketplace for himself. It was very smart.”

Although Muszynski will retire from his full-time position at PMX, he will stay on to consult through 2027, working with Joel Lunenfeld who leads PMX globally and Mark Griffin, who oversees all investment in the U.S. as chief investment officer.

Mediaplus and Publicis clients, including Banco Santander, have paused TikTok spending in wake of investigations into abuse images circulating on the video platform.

#7386. Correcting the TV ad market’s imperfect shift to streaming

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Evan Rutchik, president and Chief Strategy Officer, Viamedia.ai

The TV ad industry’s shift from linear to streaming is real, but the financial truth underlying that shift may not align with many people’s perception of the transition. That’s because the movement of ad budgets from linear to streaming has hardly been evenly paced.

Streaming’s share of ad revenue is indeed growing at major media companies, including The Walt Disney Company, Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery. But their earnings tell a different story: Linear revenue is falling faster than streaming revenue is rising. The shift is happening, but it’s not benefiting content owners as expected.

The core problem is a supply-demand imbalance. Streaming ad revenue is growing through increased volume, not higher prices. New inventory floods in, making cheap inventory abundant. As major subscription services continue to promote free ad-supported streaming tiers, the buyers’ market will only continue.

The solution is to grow demand, not increase prices. Fortunately, the ad dollars needed to support the streaming market already exist, sitting in local and midmarket budgets that haven’t been able to buy national CTV efficiently. The opportunity is to bring these advertisers into the broader video and streaming market, then develop ways to keep them investing.

Correcting the imbalance requires creating new demand, then ensuring that content quality and product performance are strong enough to eventually shift pricing dynamics.

When it comes to growth, the glut of cheap national SVOD supply may be a problem for programmers, but it’s an opportunity for local and midmarket advertisers. If the current streaming market is largely aimed at Fortune 500 companies, the industry has to consider the thousands of smaller advertisers that make steady annual investments in local linear buys. Taken collectively, these advertisers represent billions of dollars in ad budgets.

Because these advertisers have smaller budgets, many haven’t tried streaming. It is more cost-efficient for them to execute local and spot buys on linear than to invest in targeted or national streaming campaigns. These advertisers are still seeing real success reaching millions of linear viewers, so they understand the power of TV. They just need an entry point for CTV. Solving this issue would allow demand to flow steadily into the market.

Once that demand enters the market, additional steps must happen. CPMs fall when inventory is sold as undifferentiated tonnage. With platforms like Disney+ and Netflix exploring free ad-supported tiers, even more low-cost supply will enter the market. But cheap supply that doesn’t perform isn’t worth an advertiser’s investment. If that inventory doesn’t deliver results for advertisers outside the Fortune 500, those advertisers will invest their budgets elsewhere, creating another demand imbalance.

The answer lies in household addressability. While this is available on some streaming platforms, it’s still largely absent from the overall TV market, including linear. Household-level addressability turns cheap impressions into targeted impressions worth buying, even within highly premium inventory. The more information brands have about who will see their ads and whether those ads are likely to resonate with the viewer, the more they are likely to pay.

It is no secret that streaming is the future of TV, and shifting revenue numbers reflect that. To build a sustainable future for media companies and advertisers, the entire ecosystem must first provide ease of access and then addressability.

Making streaming easier to buy at scale would help solve the demand problem. Once more advertisers are in the market, addressability creates greater competition for impressions, solving the pricing imbalance.

This future is out there, provided the industry takes a few steps in the right direction.

#7384. OpenAI rewrites ChatGPT Business Hub terms after legal language raises concerns

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

OpenAI has revised the terms for its new ChatGPT Business Hub days after its launch. The change came after Digiday reported that lawyers viewed the original language as potentially limiting businesses’ ability to bring certain legal claims against the company.

Launched last week, the Business Hub gives businesses that verify their accounts direct data on how their websites appear in ChatGPT, including mentions, profile views, clicks and discovery topics. For publishers, it’s a first-party view of activity they’ve largely had to estimate.

Before the revision, Section 10 of OpenAI’s terms of service for businesses using ChatGPT appeared to require those businesses to give up certain legal claims against OpenAI, lawyers told Digiday. They said the language could apply to claims involving content published on their websites, before they signed up.

The terms included a mandatory arbitration provision and class-action waiver, potentially requiring businesses to resolve disputes individually and outside of court, Chapell said. The provision was broadly worded to cover certain claims that arose before the agreement was accepted, he explained.

Businesses had 30 days to opt out of arbitration, but if they did not, they could have faced limits on their ability to obtain evidence, pursue appeals and establish legal precedent through public court proceedings, Chapell added.

This could’ve put a chokehold on publishers that are in the midst of figuring out how to protect and monetize their content in the AI era, potentially limiting their ability to bring copyright infringement claims against OpenAI down the road.

Hours after Digiday first contacted OpenAI on Nov. 18, the terms were revised. They now include a waiver of businesses’ rights to seek a court order preventing OpenAI from using certain customer data, according to an IP and tech lawyer, who spoke on the condition of anonymity.

“There are still some restrictions here but they don’t leave a customer completely without recourse. A customer could still, even if not entirely practical, sue for monetary damages,” they said.

When approached for comment, an OpenAI spokesperson said the previous version of the terms “were not intended to cover this scenario,” and confirmed it updated the terms on Nov. 18.

“We continually evaluate the language in our Terms to make sure they are as clear as possible,” the spokesperson added in a statement. “The updated terms replace the separate ‘covered claims’ provision with language in Section 5.2 that ties the release to claims arising from a business’s Business Agent, including the use of customer data with that agent.”

One industry exec, who requested anonymity, told Digiday they advised publishers’ legal teams to be cautious around some of the potential implications of OpenAI’s terms for businesses, after they were originally published on Nov. 10.

Although the terms have been revised, several lawyers who spoke to Digiday said the original version was likely deliberate.

“This is certainly not an oversight because they put these terms out there,” said Gary Kibel, partner at law firm Davis+ Gilbert, which advises media and advertising clients. “It was incredibly intentional, and they were seeking to use this opportunity to get a release and wavier for any of these claims. It seems to be quite aggressive to do that.”

Kibel said the revised terms still require businesses to waive certain legal claims related to their use of ChatGPT Business Hub and its AI Business Agents (essentially a business-specific chatbot), but no longer extend that waiver to unrelated legal disputes with OpenAI.

OpenAI’s changes come just weeks after Meta faced major backlash from publishers over its new commercial terms, which would have allowed the company to scrape the websites of Meta Business account users. The proposed changes spurred a global, coordinated pushback from publishers, trade bodies, and government officials and regulators. In response, Meta announced it would pause the rollout until it has had time to explain the changes to key stakeholders and partners.

OpenAI’s terms revision also follows tensions between publishers and AI companies over the use of published content. Documents unsealed in The New York Times’ lawsuit against OpenAI and Microsoft two months ago revealed tech giants were treating published content as free fuel for AI products that threatened their very existence.

Whatever prompted the revision, it may do little to ease publishers’ broader concerns about how AI companies use their content, and the terms attached to new tools built around that content.

ChatGPT’s new AI visibility and click data can help inform publishers’ GEO strategies and complement third-party analytics.

Creators are tapping into their massive content libraries for syndication opportunities that pay dividends.

#7383. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

#7380. Why more brands are paying creators to make their ads

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series detailing the most important information readers need to know about the ad industry’s most complex topics. More from the series →

Brands paying influencers to make campaign creative have become key drivers of investment in the booming creator economy. Creative agencies might want to take note.

“You get the authentic, scroll-stopping content that people want to engage each with. But you also get the reach,” said Katy Ball, head of social at M&C Saatchi Sport & Entertainment.

As spending on paid social channels — and video in particular — continues to rise, demand for creative assets to appear in those ad slots is increasing.

The number of assets attached to individual campaigns has risen 30% in the last five years, according to a CreativeX study of 115,990 ad campaigns, representing $11 billion in ad spend. Per the study, just five assets receive 88% of media investment in an average campaign.

The need for more campaign assets is fueling the growth of asset-led spending, the fastest growing segment of the creator economy, according to a report published this week by research consultancy Madison & Wall.

Currently, 12% of the $112 billion spent on creators globally goes towards creators producing ads on behalf of brands, per the study. That’s expected to rise 16% every year through 2030.

The days when brands principally paid creators to rent their following are rapidly disappearing. Influencers are now media partners, ambassadors and increasingly, cogs in the creative production supply chain. And brands see creator-made ads as a solution to their social asset shortage.

“For us, the appeal is simple. Buying space in someone’s feed doesn’t mean you’ve earned their attention. Good creators understand what their audiences care about and how to give them something worth stopping for,” said Jared Kozel, co-founder and chief creative officer at agency Super Nice. “That’s valuable creative judgment to bring into an ad no matter the brand or category.”

Influencer marketing experts note that several different but related practices are included under the broader umbrella of creator-led production.

While some brands simply pay creators a fee in order to receive video or still assets for use in a campaign, whitelisting (also called “dark advertising”) has become a favored method of managing such arrangements.

In this context, an influencer grants a brand limited access to their social media accounts, allowing them to book a paid media campaign through that account, using assets made by the creator while taking advantage of discrete audience targeting.

“What we’re seeing is a lot of recutting of media spend to be allocated towards creator development in order to fund whitelisting,” said Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social.

Madison & Wall’s research suggested that funding for creative production may also be coming from PR, social or experimental budgets, as well as traditional media or production pots of money.

Another related practice, user generated content, sees brands paying regular social media users to license their content as creative assets in ad campaigns.

“They have to feed their their content engines, and they’re opening up as many different avenues as they can,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo.

The relative low cost of working with influencers, versus creative production agencies, is the primary driver behind brand spending on creator-made assets.

Although production spending is often a small fraction of an overall campaign budget, marketers are keen to squeeze every non-working (that is, non-media) dollar for all it’s worth. While that same desire is pushing some brands toward generative AI production techniques, others are turning to creators.

But as this type of spending grows, so to does the need to manage it effectively. “It’s never been more complicated to negotiate usage rights or exclusivity,” said Wedderburn.

Billion Dollar Boy, for example, recently added features to its Companion creator management tool designed to better facilitate creator-led production efforts and manage usage rights negotiations.

“The creator economy has never been more competitive, heightening the urgency for brands to understand the market context,” said Thomas Walters, the agency’s chief innocation officer and co-founder.

At the same time, increased brand spending in this area is already leading to an uptick in the fees commanded by some creators. Last year, agency practitioners reported creator rates ticking up 10-20% due to demand for expanded rights.

There’s also some evidence to show that creator-made assets perform as well as, or even better than, traditional advertising spots in a social context.

A report by System1, WPP Media and Goat published in June found that creator ads achieved 3.2X the brand-awareness lift for category leader ads as traditional advertising creative.

“We’re seeing a lot of brands say that is more valuable in terms of conversion and reach than some of the quick-and-dirty assets they previously would have relied on,” said Wedderburn.

That’s not unexpected. Audiences recognize the visual cues, language and production methods employed by creators as native to TikTok or Instagram, while clocking films shot on a closed set or using more traditional methods as foreign that same environment, a lesson that major advertisers like Nike are reluctantly absorbing.

“If programs are set up correctly, creators can deliver volume and variety faster and more cost effectively than a traditional production cycle,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer, Viral Nation. “The shift we’re seeing is brands starting to plan creators across their media budgets, rather than as a separate influencer line item alone.”

Consulting giants Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

With shareholders, an $895 billion compute bill and a costly free tier, Anthropic’s no-ads pledge may have a shelf life.

#7378. Anthropic says no to ads but Wall Street may have other ideas

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Anthropic is a public company now, which puts it on an unofficial path to an ads business.

Unofficial because the company’s official position is still firmly against ads. It went as far as spending Super Bowl money on a campaign mocking OpenAI for bringing them to ChatGPT. Its prospectus, the document pitching those public shares to investors, leaves advertisers out of the business altogether.

“Is it cynical to expect Anthropic to follow the same route? Not really. More like an educated guess,” said ad tech expert Shirley Marschall.

Besides, the company has already left the door open, she continued. Its pledge rules out sponsored links adjacent to conversations, but in the same post it says it will keep building features that help users “find, compare, or buy products, connect with businesses.” And once an agent recommends products, retail media will pay to be in those recommendations, Marschall said, before adding: “Let’s see Anthropic turn that ad revenue down,” she added.

Advertisers, for their part, will be watching closely, and with mixed feelings. On the one hand, a new media seller means a new platform to wrangle and one more set of tools and teams to learn at a time when most are still working out how to buy ChatGPT. On the other hand, Claude’s audience is also spending more and more time in those conversation threads. For marketers, that’s a hard place to stay out of for long.

“Anthropic, like any company, has a stance towards handling consumer data and inadvertently how advertising is considered,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Souza Rauto. “But monetization is a lucrative form of revenue. I’d expect Anthropic to have a change of heart. Look no further to how Apple has played this advertising game over the past few years despite being considered anti-advertising.”

Life as a public company isn’t cheap, after all, and it’s even less cheap for an AI company that has signed up for approximately $895.4 billion in compute and infrastructure commitments, some running to 2045. That’s more than 13 years of revenue at its current pace, given Q3 revenue of $17 billion. The revenue covering that bill is far less locked in since 80% of revenue is consumption-based and the S-1 says many customers can cut usage or terminate on short notice. In fact, the company has already warned that “operating margins may compress when large tranches arrive.”

Then there’s the free tier. “Free and promotional users may never convert to paid plans,” the filing admits, yet Anthropic still pays to serve every one of them. Analysts found that cost sits in sales and marketing out of sight of the headline margin. That arrangement holds as long as free users keep upgrading. If they stop, Anthropic is left with a growing audience it pays to serve and earns nothing from.

OpenAI has already shown what that audience could be worth. ChatGPT ads reached a $1 billion annualized revenue run rate less than 200 days after launching. When both companies are trading, analysts will ask Anthropic why it’s leaving money on the table.

“They’ll go after advertising, just not in the way some assume,” said Rob Webster, founder and CEO of TAU Marketing Solutions. “The prize for Anthropic isn’t ads inside Claude. Advertising is now a trillion-dollar market, and the real power sits with whatever brain coordinates it: deciding what runs, where, and why. That’s a far more valuable position than selling impressions. Anthropic wants to be a new, better Microsoft or Apple. Selling tools to businesses. Not ad opportunities.”

There are reasons, though, why Anthropic may resist the lure of ads for as long as it feasibly can.

“Trust and impartiality really matter with LLMs, particularly among enterprise customers,” said Alex Tait, founder of Entropy. “In contrast to OpenAI, Anthropic is currently approaching operating break-even without ads suggesting its existing business model could be commercially sustainable without it.”

Anthropic’s anti-ads stance is based on its perspective that once ads become part of a business’s revenue targets, the pressure to maximize engagement can start influencing product decisions. For an AI app, in this case Claude, that creates a conflict: users expect Claude to help them make decisions in their own interests, not towards whatever generates revenue for the company or its advertisers. And in its IPO prospectus, the company said that they want “Claude’s only incentive to be giving them [users] a helpful answer,” which is why they have “chosen not to introduce that pressure [ads] into Claude.”

Anthropic did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment.

As children, we play hide-and-seek. There is a kind of logic to it: If you cannot see me, then I cannot see you. As adults, and sometimes as leaders, we can fall into a similar pattern.

#7375. Future of Marketing Briefing: Consulting firms revive their marketing ambitions in the C-suite the holdcos covet

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of Marketing Briefing covers the latest in marketing for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Friday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

However the holdcos settle the question of whether the CEO route counts as due diligence, Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song got there first, and both have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

Deloitte Digital’s offer is Converge, an operating system that strings together AI agents to run marketing and sales work, from research and briefs through to campaign deployment and deal tracking. Some of the agents are Deloitte’s own. Others are ones a client already runs elsewhere, and the whole thing plugs into the tools and clout contracts a brand is already paying for. Marketing and sales versions are live, with commerce on the way, and Deloitte pitches them as one front-office system tied to revenue, which it calls “brand to cash.” Because it’s sold as a license that stretches across sales, marketing and service, it needs sign-off well above the CMO, which suits a consulting firm that’s used to having those conversations.

Accenture Song’s play in media, a market it has circled in the U.S. for years without committing. In September, it moved Melissa Fein, who built its media planning and buying business in Australia and New Zealand after leaving Initiative in 2024, to New York as U.S. media lead. This month it posted a job for a head of media strategy for the Americas. The ad describes a “new world media practice” with no legacy systems to protect, and asks for an AI-first strategist” to lead planning, build new tools and become the person senior clients call before a brief exists.

Candidates need hands-on command of econometrics, incrementality testing and attribution plus experience in big-spending categories like financial services, telecommunications, automotive and health. Those are the categories where the ANZ team won Australia Post and Mediabank. Accenture Song Ndidi Oteh has kept quiet on specifics, saying only that whatever it does in media won’t take traditional forms.

“The real prize is the conversation before the brief,” said  Steve Boehler, founder of agency consultancy the Mercer Island Group. ”If you help define the business problem, design the operating model and decide how success will be measured, you are competing long before an agency review begins.”

Both plays echo what the holdcos have spent 18 months engineering: operating systems that run a client’s marketing for it and media businesses that sell more than buying. The consultants may have the better shot at making either one stick. They already sit with the CEOs, CIOs and CFOs who sign off on systems spanning sales, marketing and service and installing that kind of software is their day job. A holdco operating system, rightly or wrongly, still reads as an agency tool, built partly to make the agency cheaper to run. Accenture also arrives in the U.S. media with no legacy buying business to protect, so it can lead with measurement and tools before it has to sell buying power.

Whether any of this translates is another matter. Consultancies have been trying to crack advertising for the best part of a decade, after all, and the media buying business still rewards a scale Accenture doesn’t yet have in the U.S. Then again, now is arguably as good a time as any for them to try, if not better. With Omnicom and IPG combined, big advertisers are down to three holdcos, and some are skipping the pitch one so a credible alternative has rarely been more useful. AI is also pushing decisions about how marketing works into rooms where the CFO and CIO sit, which consultancies have been in for years. And a C-suite that takes a closer look at marketing will start asking how agencies make their money, from data fees to principal media, which is an awkward question for any firm with a buying business to defend.

“The consultancies may have an advantage in getting invited to design the system,” said Boehler. “That is not the same as proving they can run marketing better. The client still needs evidence of better decisions, better work and better business outcomes, not just fewer handoffs.”

All of which makes the Publicis-WPP episode look a little quaint, partly because it was never quite the fight it was billed as. Sadoun spent months telling investors that Publicis was winning big accounts without a pitch. Rose, asked about it by an analyst, called it “this pitchless CEO handshake thing that I keep getting asked about” and said decisions that big deserve careful consideration. Publicis answered with an open letter treating that line as her theory, and the coverage did the rest. Somewhere along the way, a question about how clients choose agencies turned into a row about manners.

What got lost was the shift both CEOs were describing. Big advertisers are increasingly buying data, identity and AI infrastructure, which a six-week parade of slide decks was never built to judge, so some now test one partner in depth instead of auditioning several. Those decisions are also moving up the organization chart, to executives who sign off on systems not campaigns. That’s how consultancies have always been bought. Deloitte and Accenture are built for long, technical evaluations run by CFOs and CIOs, and while the holdcos; remarks were being turned into a grudge march one launched a system that needs the c-suite’s signature and the other started building a U.S. media practice designed to win it.

“Client CEOs and CMOs pay attention to CEOs of holdcos, especially when a transition in agency leadership occurs and the agency CEO conducts outreach to key clients,” said Gartner analyst Jay Wilson. “But most recognize that while agency CEOs may set a direction for the agency, that direction may be slow to take hold – or never take hold – within the agency teams doing the actual day-to- day work.  A strong relationship with, or aversion to, an agency chief executive is usually more consequential at smaller, independent agencies.”

Whatever gets agreed in the c-suite, someone still has to do the work.

“The consultants don’t have to copy the incumbent model to compete,” said Boehler. “But they still have to do the work: plan, negotiate, deliver, reconcile and prove results. A different model is a proposition until clients experience the difference.”

Cinema admissions have climbed back on the strength of a fuller blockbuster slate and much of the ad money that shifted to digital during lockdowns stayed put. Most screens still sell their preshow through sales houses that have always sold offline.

Mikkel Hagedorn, a WPP and Omnicom alum, has spent five or six years building a platform that lets buyers book cinema spots programmatically, target them against specific films and count impressions straight off ticket sales. The company is live in Denmark and Finland, where sales houses have handed it two minutes of a 15-minute preshow at a base CPM of around €75. Hagedorn’s bet is that exhibitors can bring cinema into the digital ad market while keeping the premium pricing newspapers gave away when they went programmatic.

His company, called Cinemataztic, gives buyers two ways to purchase inventory: first, agencies already running programmatic campaigns can reach the inventory through supply-side platforms and demand-side platforms such as Smartclip Google’s DV360 respectively, which lets cinema sit inside budgets that were going to be spread across other channels anyway, Advertisers who would rather skip the intermediary fees can book directly through Hagedorn’s own interface.

Either way, the buyer can pick what individual film they want to target, so a brand could run only against the upcoming Avengers: Doomsday movie if it likes. The impressions they buy come straight from exhibitors’ ticketing systems, which means one ticket sold counts as one impression. There are “no multipliers here,” said Hagedorn, a dig at the audience multipliers that inflate numbers elsewhere in media.

There’s also a performance bent to the inventory. Audiences log into a pre-show game, which feeds real-time targeting data and lets advertisers push vouchers to their phones. Hagedorn pointed to a Specsavers campaign as an example. On lead generation campaigns, he claimed an average click-through rate of 10%, a figure display buyers stopped dreaming about years ago.

So far a lot of the money has come from digital agencies edging up the funnel into brand work. Hagedorn said he has seen little cannibalisation of advertisers still buying cinema the old way. He said the business has grown 50% a year and forecasts the same for next year, when the company expands into Sweden, Thailand and a third, bigger market he wouldn’t share.

Even so, the programmatication of cinema will be a slow burn. Advertising is still its third-largest revenue line after tickets and concessions. Most hand it entirely to sales houses on contracts of three to five years and only learn that demand is falling when the next guarantee shrinks. Hagedorn has been warning them about this for years. “They cannot stay offline forever,” he said.

Brands that gatecrash cultural moments tend to stick to tournaments and royal weddings, and they rarely pick a game that lets players mug strangers in a fake Florida. Grand Theft Auto, the culture-warping franchise, is the exception, and its sixth installment is drawing the kind of guerilla marketing normally reserved for FIFA and the Olympics.

Anyone trying to keep up can follow the running tally at GTA6Brands.com, an independent tracker run by Mike Litman that logs a new stunt each day for his GTA 6 Marketing News podcast. Hardly anything in culture still sets off that kind of scramble across so many brands at once, and with GTA the explanation is scale.

“I’ve tracked over 290 brand moves around GTA 6 so far, including around 130 unofficial activations,” said Litman. “That’s a remarkable amount of marketing activity orbiting a product launch, particularly when so many of those brands have no formal relationship with Rockstar. What’s interesting is the range of brands getting involved.”

With media this fragmented, marketers have a shortlist of moments when a mass audience shows up at the same time, and most of those moments involve a ball. GTA earned its spot on that list over 13 years. When GTA 5 launched in 2013 it made $1 billion in three days, which owner Take Two called a rate faster than any other video game, film or other entertainment product has ever managed. For context, Marvel’s “The Avengers” movie took 19 days to reach that number. In the years since it launched, the game has since surpassed 230 million units sold worldwide across three console generations. The wait for the sequel turned into an event of its own. GTA 6’s second trailer received 475 million views across all platforms within 24 hours of its release, beating the 365 million in 24 hours for Marvel’s Deadpool & Wolverine film. Take Two has issued $8 billion for its 2027 guidance for the fiscal year the game launches in. For a brand that wants a crowd all watching the same thing at once, that’s about as close to a World Cup final as entertainment gets, and nobody has to get past FIFA’s lawyers.

“I think the fascinating thing is that GTA 6 has achieved something usually reserved for the World Cup or Super Bowl, but without the stadium, the broadcast rights or the traditional sponsorship machinery,” said Litman. “It’s become a cultural event in its own right. And brands aren’t waiting for permission to participate.”

A few did get permission, though. Rockstar’s list of official partners is short and made up mostly of platforms. Sony signed an exclusive marketing deal, which put a “Plays Best on PS5” tag on the game’s official channels. Netflix got the “Extended Look” trailer six trailers before YouTube in August, in what Take Two boss Strauss Zelnick called a “groundbreaking partnership”. YouTube is running a 24-hour launch live livestream from its own channel, and the Miami Heat have redesigned court, custom uniforms and Vice City merchandise.

“Nike is going back to its roots and what built the brand: cultural relevance through people who have the trust of a community. It is a bold choice when most advertising pitches are about AI. Nike uses AI too, as a tool, but clearly shows it understands that what stays scarce is trust. Trust comes from identity and proximity: who is speaking, and how close they are to the community listening. It also shows what creators and athletes must build to ask a brand like Nike for more than just a campaign fee. Most of them are offering access to an audience that belongs to a social or tech platform, targeted and measured with the data that the platform makes available. Global stars aside, only those who directly own the relationship with their audience and their data can negotiate a share of the long-term value.”

— Alessandro De Zanche, founder of media consultancy ADZ Strategies on Nike’s bet on creators to drive growth.

#7363. Executive Assistant at Offchain Labs
